A 2-dimension linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy decision-making method with application to unmanned aerial vehicle contribution assessment
Authors: Fei Gao, Weikai He
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
Assessing the contribution of an unmanned aerial vehicle to the effectiveness of a swarm is a challenging problem, as it depends heavily on expert judgments that are often subjective, imprecise, and expressed with varying levels of confidence. Existing decision-making methods can capture evaluative uncertainty but generally fail to represent the reliability of those evaluations within a unified framework. To address this gap, this study introduces a 2-dimension linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy variable, which simultaneously represents an expert’s linguistic evaluation and the confidence attached to that evaluation under the Pythagorean fuzzy condition. The fundamental operational rules, score and accuracy functions, and aggregation operators for 2-dimension linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy variables are developed, and their algebraic properties are formally established. Building on this representation, a multi-criteria decision-making method is proposed for unmanned aerial vehicle contribution assessment. A practical case study demonstrates that the method produces results that are consistent with established approaches, strongly correlated in ranking performance, and sensitive to differences in expert confidence, thereby providing both reliability and interpretability. Nevertheless, the current study assumes fixed linguistic term sets and a static confidence dimension, and the case study is limited to a small number of evaluation criteria. Future research will address calibration of linguistic scales, dynamic updating of confidence, and broader validation across domains. Overall, the proposed approach offers a structured and reliable tool for evaluating unmanned aerial vehicle contributions in swarm operations and enriches the methodological foundations for decision-making under uncertainty.

Introduction

Unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) have transformed a wide range of industries by providing versatile and efficient solutions to tasks that were previously challenging or impossible1–3. In the military sector, UAVs are deployed for surveillance, reconnaissance, and precision missions, thereby reducing risks to human pilots4. In agriculture, UAVs enable precision farming by monitoring crop health, applying pesticides, and managing irrigation with high accuracy5. In logistics, UAVs are increasingly utilized for last-mile delivery, particularly in remote or hard-to-access areas, offering a cost-effective and timely alternative to conventional methods6. These diverse applications highlight the growing importance of UAVs in both commercial and governmental operations, which in turn drives the need for rigorous evaluation methods to ensure that the most suitable UAVs are selected for specific missions.

Recently, the concept of UAV swarms has gained significant attention as a promising mode of UAV application7. A UAV swarm denotes a coordinated group of UAVs collaborating to achieve an operational goal, where numerous influencing factors-some difficult to quantify precisely-affect overall performance8,9. Within such swarms, the contribution degree of a UAV represents its influence on the swarm’s overall operational effectiveness. Evaluating UAV contribution is crucial for optimizing swarm performance and adapting UAV configurations10,11. However, in practice, relevant information may be incomplete, inaccessible, or inherently uncertain. Consequently, evaluations often rely on expert groups, whose judgments are typically expressed in uncertain, fuzzy, and linguistic terms due to the complex and ambiguous nature of the problem12–14. This makes it appropriate to formulate UAV contribution assessment as a multi-criteria decision-making (MCDM) problem under uncertainty.

In MCDM problems, experts provide judgments based on their cognitive abilities, and the goal is to identify the most appropriate alternative from the available options. Traditional evaluation methods, while effective in some contexts, may not capture the full spectrum of uncertainty and the linguistic subtleties inherent in human decision-making15–19. To address this, Zadeh20 introduced the fuzzy set (FS) as a means of representing cognitive information, which has since attracted widespread attention in various fields21–23. Building on FS, Yager24 introduced the Pythagorean fuzzy set (PFS), where the sum of the squares of the membership and non-membership degrees does not exceed 1, thereby offering a more flexible representation of assessments25–27.

Although PFSs are powerful, they rely on precise numerical input. In many real-world contexts, however, experts prefer to express evaluations linguistically using terms such as “good,” “moderate,” or “poor”28–31. To address this, Zadeh32 introduced the notion of linguistic variables, which use natural language to express qualitative judgments and thus provide a more flexible and natural way for experts to convey their assessments under uncertainty and subjectivity33–35. Combining linguistic variables with the Pythagorean structure, Garg36 proposed the linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy set (LPFS), in which membership and non-membership are expressed in linguistic terms rather than crisp values37–40. While LPFS effectively models evaluative uncertainty, it does not capture the expert’s confidence or reliability in those judgments.

To better incorporate reliability, Zhu et al.41 introduced the 2-dimension linguistic variable (2DLV). In this framework, type I information represents the linguistic evaluation of an attribute, while type II information represents the subjective confidence in that evaluation. Compared with one-dimension fuzzy sets that represent only evaluative information42,43, 2DLVs provide a richer description by also encoding reliability, and have been applied in various MCDM problems44–46. Building on this, Verma and Merigó47 proposed the 2-dimension linguistic intuitionistic fuzzy variable (2DLIFV), where both dimensions are expressed using linguistic intuitionistic fuzzy sets (LIFSs). This model has been applied in several decision-making contexts, such as the 2DLIF-PROMETHEE and 2DLIF-MULTIMOORA methods48,49.

Despite these advances, a distinct research gap remains. On the one hand, LPFS provides flexibility through the Pythagorean condition but cannot model reliability. On the other hand, 2DLV and 2DLIFV explicitly incorporate reliability but are either too simplistic or constrained by the more restrictive intuitionistic condition. In particular, 2DLIFV may lack sufficient flexibility in highly uncertain contexts where expert hesitation is significant. This highlights the need for an information representation model that combines the expressive power of LPFS with the reliability dimension of 2DLV, thereby supporting richer and more realistic decision modeling.

Addressing this gap is the main motivation for this study. The motivation is threefold: To develop a more comprehensive and expressive information model by integrating the LPFS with the 2-dimension linguistic framework. The LPFS, which extends intuitionistic fuzzy logic by allowing the sum of the squares of membership and non-membership degrees to be less than or equal to 1, provides a richer structure for capturing uncertainty. Integrating this with a two-dimensional framework offers higher flexibility in representing and managing uncertain information, allowing for more accurate and context-sensitive decision-making.To enhance decision-making in complex scenarios such as UAV contribution assessment, which are characterized by high uncertainty. Such problems require capturing subtle differences in judgment that traditional methods may not effectively represent. The LPFS’s broader capability to model degrees of membership and non-membership enhances the ability to differentiate between closely related options, making it suitable for this challenging domain.To provide a more robust tool than existing hybrid methods like 2DLIFV. The need for a tool that can handle the granularity and detail of UAV assessment is a key driver. The LPFS adeptly expresses uncertain judgments, while the 2-dimension framework adds the crucial layer of judgment reliability. By combining these strengths, this study proposes a more effective approach to tackle the challenges inherent in UAV contribution assessment.Motivated by the above analysis, this study introduces a novel information representation model, termed the 2-dimension linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy set (2DLPFS), by combining the LPFS and the 2DLV. The 2DLPFS can simultaneously represent type I information (the evaluative judgment) and type II information (the confidence in that judgment) within the Pythagorean linguistic framework, thereby enhancing the ability to model complex and hesitant cognitive information. Building upon this representation, a multi-criteria decision-making approach for UAV contribution assessment is constructed.

By combining these strengths, this study proposes a more effective approach to tackle the challenges inherent in UAV contribution assessment.

The LPFS adeptly expresses uncertain judgments, while the 2-dimension framework adds the crucial layer of judgment reliability. By combining these strengths, this study proposes a more effective approach to tackle the challenges inherent in UAV contribution assessment.

To further illustrate the motivation for the proposed model, it is useful to briefly consider the geometrical interpretation of fuzzy sets and their extensions. A classical fuzzy set can be visualized on the unit interval, where each element corresponds to a single membership degree in [0, 1]. Intuitionistic fuzzy sets extend this concept by introducing both membership and non-membership degrees constrained by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu +\nu \le 1$$\end{document}, which geometrically defines a feasible triangular region in the unit square, with the distance to the boundary line reflecting the degree of hesitation.

Pythagorean fuzzy sets generalize this by replacing the linear constraint with a quadratic one, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^2+\nu ^2 \le 1$$\end{document}, defining a quarter unit circle in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mu ,\nu )$$\end{document} plane. This circular admissible region allows larger simultaneous values of membership and non-membership, thereby offering greater flexibility in capturing hesitation compared with intuitionistic fuzzy sets. Linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy sets embed this geometry into a linguistic scale, associating admissible points with qualitative terms such as “low,” “moderate,” or “high.”

The proposed 2DLPFS extends this geometric intuition further. In addition to the quarter-circle domain representing the evaluative judgment (type I information), a second orthogonal quarter-circle domain is introduced to represent the reliability or confidence of that judgment (type II information). A 2-dimension linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy variable (2DLPFV) thus corresponds to a pair of admissible points, one in each domain, jointly capturing both what an expert believes and how strongly that belief is held. This richer representational space avoids the information loss inherent in one-dimension models and forms the basis of the reliability-aware decision-making framework developed in this study. The main contributions of this study are as follows: The 2DLPFV is formally defined to encode both the evaluative judgment and the associated reliability of expert opinions. This addresses a fundamental limitation of single-axis models that cannot distinguish between these two types of information, and it offers greater geometric flexibility than existing two-axis intuitionistic models by utilizing the less restrictive Pythagorean condition.A set of basic operational rules and four aggregation operators are established for 2DLPFVs: the weighted average (2DLPFWA), the ordered weighted average (2DLPFOWA), the weighted geometric (2DLPFWG), and the ordered weighted geometric (2DLPFOWG). These operators are shown to satisfy essential properties such as closure, boundedness, and idempotency, thereby ensuring their mathematical soundness and interpretability within the 2DLPFS framework.Building on these constructs, a multi-criteria decision-making method is developed for UAV contribution assessment under uncertainty. The method applies expert evaluations expressed in the 2DLPFS framework to derive reliability-aware rankings of alternatives. A case study demonstrates that the proposed method produces stable and interpretable results that are sensitive to expert confidence, highlighting its practical value for complex engineering assessments where the reliability of judgments is crucial.To illustrate the proposed method, a numerical example involving UAV selection based on contribution assessment is presented. Comparative analyses with other methods are conducted to validate and showcase the effectiveness of the proposed approach.

The 2DLPFV is formally defined to encode both the evaluative judgment and the associated reliability of expert opinions. This addresses a fundamental limitation of single-axis models that cannot distinguish between these two types of information, and it offers greater geometric flexibility than existing two-axis intuitionistic models by utilizing the less restrictive Pythagorean condition.

The 2DLPFV is formally defined to encode both the evaluative judgment and the associated reliability of expert opinions. This addresses a fundamental limitation of single-axis models that cannot distinguish between these two types of information, and it offers greater geometric flexibility than existing two-axis intuitionistic models by utilizing the less restrictive Pythagorean condition.

A set of basic operational rules and four aggregation operators are established for 2DLPFVs: the weighted average (2DLPFWA), the ordered weighted average (2DLPFOWA), the weighted geometric (2DLPFWG), and the ordered weighted geometric (2DLPFOWG). These operators are shown to satisfy essential properties such as closure, boundedness, and idempotency, thereby ensuring their mathematical soundness and interpretability within the 2DLPFS framework.

A set of basic operational rules and four aggregation operators are established for 2DLPFVs: the weighted average (2DLPFWA), the ordered weighted average (2DLPFOWA), the weighted geometric (2DLPFWG), and the ordered weighted geometric (2DLPFOWG). These operators are shown to satisfy essential properties such as closure, boundedness, and idempotency, thereby ensuring their mathematical soundness and interpretability within the 2DLPFS framework.

Building on these constructs, a multi-criteria decision-making method is developed for UAV contribution assessment under uncertainty. The method applies expert evaluations expressed in the 2DLPFS framework to derive reliability-aware rankings of alternatives. A case study demonstrates that the proposed method produces stable and interpretable results that are sensitive to expert confidence, highlighting its practical value for complex engineering assessments where the reliability of judgments is crucial.

Building on these constructs, a multi-criteria decision-making method is developed for UAV contribution assessment under uncertainty. The method applies expert evaluations expressed in the 2DLPFS framework to derive reliability-aware rankings of alternatives. A case study demonstrates that the proposed method produces stable and interpretable results that are sensitive to expert confidence, highlighting its practical value for complex engineering assessments where the reliability of judgments is crucial.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Some basic concepts are briefly reviewed in “Preliminaries”. In “2-dimension linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy sets”, we give the definition of 2DLPFVs, and present the operational rules, score function and accuracy function of 2DLPFVs. In “2-dimension linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy aggregation operators”, we introduce a series of aggregation operators for 2DLPFVs and prove their properties. A novel MCDM method with 2DLPFVs for UAV contribution assessment is proposed in “A novel multi-criteria decision-making method for UAV contribution assessment” on the basis of the operators. In “Case study”, we present a numerical example to illustrate the process of the proposed method. Some discussions and managerial implications are provided in “Discussions and managerial implications”. Finally, “Conclusion” concludes the paper.

Definition 2.2

50 Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S=\{s_0, s_1,\dots , s_t\}$$\end{document} be a linguistic term set (LTS) with odd cardinality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t+1$$\end{document}, and any linguistic term (LT) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_i\in S$$\end{document} denotes a possible value for linguistic variable. As the LTS is a discrete set, Xu51 introduced the continuous linguistic term set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{S}}=\{s_a|s_0\le s_a\le s_t,a\in [0,t]\}$$\end{document} that meets the above properties. Definition 2.3

36 Let X be a fixed set and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{S}}=\{s_a|s_0\le s_a\le s_t,a\in [0,t]\}$$\end{document} be a continuous linguistic terms set. A linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy set (LPFS) is defined as:2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} A=\left\{ \left\langle x, s_{\theta }(x), s_{\sigma }(x)\right\rangle |x\in X\right\} \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_{\theta }(x), s_{\sigma }(x)\in {\hat{S}}$$\end{document} denote the membership degree and non-membership degree of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in X$$\end{document} to A, respectively, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \theta ,\sigma \le t$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^2+\sigma ^2\le t^2$$\end{document}. The linguistic indeterminacy degree of x to A is defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_{\pi }(x)=s_{\sqrt{t^2-\theta ^2-\sigma ^2}}$$\end{document}. For a given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in X$$\end{document}, the pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle s_{\theta }(x), s_{\sigma }(x)\right\rangle$$\end{document} is called the linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy variable (LPFV), which is simplified as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A=\left\langle s_{\theta }, s_{\sigma }\right\rangle$$\end{document}. This is particularly valuable when precise quantification is impractical. For example, an expert might say a UAV’s sensing capability is “very good” with non-membership “slightly poor”. These terms are converted into linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy values, capturing both evaluation and uncertainty.

Definition 2.4

36 Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A=\left\langle s_{\theta }, s_{\sigma }\right\rangle$$\end{document} be a LPFV with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_{\theta }, s_{\sigma }\in {\hat{S}}$$\end{document}, then the score function of A is defined as:3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} S(A)=s_{\sqrt{(t^2+\theta ^2-\sigma ^2)/2}} \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}and the accuracy function of A is defined as:4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} H(A)=s_{\sqrt{\theta ^2+\sigma ^2}} \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Definition 2.5

36 Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A=\left\langle s_{\theta }, s_{\sigma }\right\rangle$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1=\left\langle s_{\theta _1}, s_{\sigma _1}\right\rangle$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_2=\left\langle s_{\theta _2}, s_{\sigma _2}\right\rangle$$\end{document} be three LPFVs with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_{\theta }, s_{\sigma }, s_{\theta _1}, s_{\sigma _1}, s_{\theta _2}, s_{\sigma _2}\in {\hat{S}}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda >0$$\end{document} be a real number, then the operational rules on LPFVs are as follows: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1\oplus A_2=\left\langle s_{\sqrt{\theta _1^2+\theta _2^2-\frac{\theta _1^2\theta _2^2}{t^2}}}, s_{\frac{\sigma _1\sigma _2}{t}}\right\rangle$$\end{document}.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda A=\left\langle s_{t\sqrt{1-\left( 1-\frac{\theta ^2}{t^2}\right) ^{\lambda }}}, s_{t\left( \frac{\sigma }{t}\right) ^{\lambda }}\right\rangle$$\end{document}.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^{\lambda }=\left\langle s_{t\left( \frac{\theta }{t}\right) ^{\lambda }}, s_{t\sqrt{1-\left( 1-\frac{\sigma ^2}{t^2}\right) ^{\lambda }}}\right\rangle$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1\oplus A_2=\left\langle s_{\sqrt{\theta _1^2+\theta _2^2-\frac{\theta _1^2\theta _2^2}{t^2}}}, s_{\frac{\sigma _1\sigma _2}{t}}\right\rangle$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1\oplus A_2=\left\langle s_{\sqrt{\theta _1^2+\theta _2^2-\frac{\theta _1^2\theta _2^2}{t^2}}}, s_{\frac{\sigma _1\sigma _2}{t}}\right\rangle$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1\otimes A_2=\left\langle s_{\frac{\theta _1\theta _2}{t}}, s_{\sqrt{\sigma _1^2+\sigma _2^2-\frac{\sigma _1^2\sigma _2^2}{t^2}}}\right\rangle$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1\otimes A_2=\left\langle s_{\frac{\theta _1\theta _2}{t}}, s_{\sqrt{\sigma _1^2+\sigma _2^2-\frac{\sigma _1^2\sigma _2^2}{t^2}}}\right\rangle$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda A=\left\langle s_{t\sqrt{1-\left( 1-\frac{\theta ^2}{t^2}\right) ^{\lambda }}}, s_{t\left( \frac{\sigma }{t}\right) ^{\lambda }}\right\rangle$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda A=\left\langle s_{t\sqrt{1-\left( 1-\frac{\theta ^2}{t^2}\right) ^{\lambda }}}, s_{t\left( \frac{\sigma }{t}\right) ^{\lambda }}\right\rangle$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^{\lambda }=\left\langle s_{t\left( \frac{\theta }{t}\right) ^{\lambda }}, s_{t\sqrt{1-\left( 1-\frac{\sigma ^2}{t^2}\right) ^{\lambda }}}\right\rangle$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^{\lambda }=\left\langle s_{t\left( \frac{\theta }{t}\right) ^{\lambda }}, s_{t\sqrt{1-\left( 1-\frac{\sigma ^2}{t^2}\right) ^{\lambda }}}\right\rangle$$\end{document}.

Definition 2.6

41 Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S=\{\dot{s}_i|i=0,1,\dots ,t\}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H=\{\ddot{h}_j|j=0,1,\dots ,\tau \}$$\end{document} be two LTSs. A 2-dimension linguistic variable (2DLV) is defined as:5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} r=(\dot{s}_i,\ddot{h}_j) \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{s}_i\in \dot{S}$$\end{document} denotes the expert’s assessment of the alternative, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ddot{h}_j\in {\ddot{H}}$$\end{document} denotes the expert’s assessment on the reliability of the given result. This explicitly separates “what the expert thinks” from “how strongly the expert believes it.” For example, Expert A may evaluate a UAV as “moderately reliable” with “high confidence,” while Expert B may also give “moderately reliable” but with “low confidence.” Traditional models treat these as the same, whereas 2DLVs capture the difference.

2-dimension linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy sets

The definition of 2DLPFS is given as follows:

Definition 3.1

Let X be a fixed set, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S=\{\dot{s}_i|i=0,1,\dots ,t\}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H=\{\ddot{h}_j|j=0,1,\dots ,\tau \}$$\end{document} be two LTSs. Then the 2-dimension linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy set (2DLPFS) R in X is defined as:6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} R=\left\{ \left\langle x,{\tilde{r}}(x)\right\rangle |x\in X\right\} \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}(x)=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{\theta }(x),\dot{s}_{\sigma }(x)\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\zeta }(x),\ddot{h}_{\eta }(x)\right\rangle \right)$$\end{document} is called a 2-dimension linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy variable (2DLPFV), with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle \dot{s}_{\theta }(x),\dot{s}_{\sigma }(x)\right\rangle$$\end{document} is the I class linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy information denoting the linguistic information of the expert’s assessment about the alternative, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\zeta }(x),\ddot{h}_{\eta }(x)\right\rangle$$\end{document} is the II class linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy information denoting the linguistic information of the expert’s assessment on the reliability of the given result. For simplicity, the 2DLPFV can be simplified as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{\theta },\dot{s}_{\sigma }\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\zeta },\ddot{h}_{\eta }\right\rangle \right)$$\end{document}. For any 2DLPFV, there is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \dot{s}_{\theta },\dot{s}_{\sigma }\le t$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \ddot{h}_{\zeta },\ddot{h}_{\eta }\le \tau$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{s}_{\theta }^2+\dot{s}_{\sigma }^2\le t^2$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ddot{h}_{\zeta }^2+\ddot{h}_{\eta }^2\le \tau ^2$$\end{document}.

Similar to the linguistic term, in order to minimize the loss of linguistic information, the discrete linguistic term sets of 2-dimension linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy information are extended to continuous linguistic term sets such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{s}_{\theta },\dot{s}_{\sigma }\in {\hat{S}}=\{\dot{s}_i|i\in [0,t]\}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ddot{h}_{\zeta },\ddot{h}_{\eta }\in {\hat{H}}=\{\ddot{h}_j|j\in [0,\tau ]\}$$\end{document}.

To compare any given two 2DLPFVs, the score function and accuracy function are defined as follows:

Definition 3.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{\theta _{{\tilde{r}}}},\dot{s}_{\sigma _{{\tilde{r}}}}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}}},\ddot{h}_{\eta _{{\tilde{r}}}}\right\rangle \right)$$\end{document} be a 2DLPFV. The score function of A is defined as:7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} S({\tilde{r}})=u_{\sqrt{\frac{t^2+\theta _{{\tilde{r}}}^2-\sigma _{\tilde{r}}^2}{2t^2}}\times \sqrt{\frac{\tau ^2+\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}}^2-\eta _{\tilde{r}}^2}{2\tau ^2}}} \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}and the accuracy function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}$$\end{document} is defined as:8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} H({\tilde{r}})=u_{\sqrt{\frac{\theta _{{\tilde{r}}}^2+\sigma _{\tilde{r}}^2}{t^2}}\times \sqrt{\frac{\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}}^2+\eta _{\tilde{r}}^2}{\tau ^2}}} \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{\psi }$$\end{document} is a linguistic term defined on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{U}}=\{u_d|d\in [0,1]\}$$\end{document}.

Based on the score and accuracy functions, for any two 2DLPFVs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_2$$\end{document}, the following comparison laws are defined: if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S({\tilde{r}}_1)>S({\tilde{r}}_2)$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1\succ {\tilde{r}}_2$$\end{document}.if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S({\tilde{r}}_1)=S({\tilde{r}}_2)$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H({\tilde{r}}_1)>H({\tilde{r}}_2)$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1\succ {\tilde{r}}_2$$\end{document}.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H({\tilde{r}}_1)=H({\tilde{r}}_2)$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1={\tilde{r}}_2$$\end{document}.The ranges and behaviors of these functions merit further discussion. Given the linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy condition, which requires \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \theta ^2+\sigma ^2\le t^2$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \zeta ^2+\eta ^2\le \tau ^2$$\end{document}, the subscript d of the resulting linguistic term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_d$$\end{document} for both the score and accuracy functions is bounded within the interval [0, 1]. The score function, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S({\tilde{r}})$$\end{document}, can be interpreted as the overall positive strength of the 2DLPFV. It reaches its maximum value when both dimensions represent complete positive membership (e.g., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\theta ,\sigma )=(t,0)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\zeta ,\eta )=(\tau ,0)$$\end{document}) and approaches zero as non-membership dominates. In contrast, the accuracy function, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H({\tilde{r}})$$\end{document}, represents the degree of certainty in the assessment. Its value increases as the sum of the squares of the membership and non-membership degrees grows, indicating a lower degree of hesitancy. This clarifies the logic of the comparison laws: alternatives are primarily ranked by their positive strength (score), and any ties are resolved by preferring the alternative with the more confident assessment (accuracy).

if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S({\tilde{r}}_1)>S({\tilde{r}}_2)$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1\succ {\tilde{r}}_2$$\end{document}.

if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S({\tilde{r}}_1)>S({\tilde{r}}_2)$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1\succ {\tilde{r}}_2$$\end{document}.

if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S({\tilde{r}}_1)=S({\tilde{r}}_2)$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H({\tilde{r}}_1)>H({\tilde{r}}_2)$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1\succ {\tilde{r}}_2$$\end{document}.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H({\tilde{r}}_1)=H({\tilde{r}}_2)$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1={\tilde{r}}_2$$\end{document}.

if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S({\tilde{r}}_1)=S({\tilde{r}}_2)$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H({\tilde{r}}_1)>H({\tilde{r}}_2)$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1\succ {\tilde{r}}_2$$\end{document}.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H({\tilde{r}}_1)=H({\tilde{r}}_2)$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1={\tilde{r}}_2$$\end{document}.

Example 3.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{5},\dot{s}_{2}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{2},\ddot{h}_{3}\right\rangle \right)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_2=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{4},\dot{s}_{2}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{1},\ddot{h}_{3}\right\rangle \right)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_3=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{3},\dot{s}_{5}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{2},\ddot{h}_{2}\right\rangle \right)$$\end{document},, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_4=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{4},\dot{s}_{4}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{1},\ddot{h}_{2}\right\rangle \right)$$\end{document} be 2DLPFVs derived from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{S}}=\{\dot{s}_\xi |\dot{s}_0\le \dot{s}_\xi \le \dot{s}_6,\xi \in [0,6]\}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{H}}=\{\ddot{h}_\psi |\ddot{h}_0\le \ddot{h}_\psi \le \ddot{h}_4,\psi \in [0,4]\}$$\end{document}. Then by applying Eqs. (7) and (8), there is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{gathered} S({\tilde{r}}_1)=s_{\sqrt{0.2721}}=s_{0.5217} \\ S({\tilde{r}}_2)=s_{\sqrt{0.1667}}=s_{0.4082} \\ S({\tilde{r}}_3)=s_{\sqrt{0.1389}}=s_{0.3727} \\ S({\tilde{r}}_4)=s_{\sqrt{0.2031}}=s_{0.4507} \end{gathered} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Thus, the ranking order of these four 2DLPFVs are obtained as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1\succ {\tilde{r}}_4\succ {\tilde{r}}_2\succ {\tilde{r}}_3$$\end{document}.

Next, we define the basic operations between 2DLPFVs as follows:

Definition 3.3

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1<{\tilde{r}}_2$$\end{document} if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_1}<\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_2}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _{{\tilde{r}}_1}>\sigma _{{\tilde{r}}_2}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_1}<\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _{{\tilde{r}}_1}>\eta _{{\tilde{r}}_2}$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1^c=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{t-\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_1}},\dot{s}_{t-\sigma _{{\tilde{r}}_1}}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\tau -\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_1}},\ddot{h}_{\tau -\eta _{{\tilde{r}}_1}}\right\rangle \right)$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1^c$$\end{document} is the complement of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1\cup {\tilde{r}}_2=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{\max \{\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_1},\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_2}\}},\dot{s}_{\min \{\sigma _{{\tilde{r}}_1},\sigma _{{\tilde{r}}_2}\}}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\max \{\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_1},\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_2}\}},\ddot{h}_{\min \{\eta _{{\tilde{r}}_1},\eta _{{\tilde{r}}_1}\}}\right\rangle \right)$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1\cap {\tilde{r}}_2=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{\min \{\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_1},\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_2}\}},\dot{s}_{\max \{\sigma _{{\tilde{r}}_1},\sigma _{{\tilde{r}}_2}\}}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\min \{\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_1},\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_2}\}},\ddot{h}_{\max \{\eta _{{\tilde{r}}_1},\eta _{{\tilde{r}}_1}\}}\right\rangle \right)$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1\cap {\tilde{r}}_2=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{\min \{\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_1},\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_2}\}},\dot{s}_{\max \{\sigma _{{\tilde{r}}_1},\sigma _{{\tilde{r}}_2}\}}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\min \{\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_1},\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_2}\}},\ddot{h}_{\max \{\eta _{{\tilde{r}}_1},\eta _{{\tilde{r}}_1}\}}\right\rangle \right)$$\end{document}

Then, the operational rules between 2DLPFVs are defined as follows:

Definition 3.4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_1}},\dot{s}_{\sigma _{{\tilde{r}}_1}}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_1}},\ddot{h}_{\eta _{\tilde{r}_1}}\right\rangle \right)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_2=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_2}},\dot{s}_{\sigma _{\tilde{r}_2}}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_2}},\ddot{h}_{\eta _{{\tilde{r}}_2}}\right\rangle \right)$$\end{document} be two 2DLPFVs, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda >0$$\end{document} be a real number, then there is: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1\oplus {\tilde{r}}_2=\left( _{{\tilde{r}}_2}}{t}}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\sqrt{\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_1}^2+\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_2}^2-\frac{\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_1}^2\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_2}^2}{\tau ^2}}},\ddot{h}_{\frac{\eta _{{\tilde{r}}_1}\eta _{{\tilde{r}}_2}}{\tau }}\right\rangle \right)$$\end{document}.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} Remark 3.1

These operations reduce to their LPFS counterparts when the reliability axis is fixed at maximal certainty. The novelty is that both evaluative and reliability information are updated simultaneously, so unreliable judgments cannot dominate the aggregate result.

Theorem 3.1

For any two 2DLPFVs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_i=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_i}},\dot{s}_{\sigma _{\tilde{r}_i}}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_i}},\ddot{h}_{\eta _{{\tilde{r}}_i}}\right\rangle \right) \ (i=1,2)$$\end{document}, the values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1\oplus {\tilde{r}}_2$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1\otimes {\tilde{r}}_2$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda {\tilde{r}}_1$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_1^{\lambda }$$\end{document} are stills 2DLPFVs, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda >0$$\end{document}.

Proof

The proof of Theorem 3.1 is presented in A. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Proof

The proof of Theorem 3.2 is presented in B. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

2-dimension linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy aggregation operators

In this section, a series of foundational aggregation operators for 2-dimension linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy variables are introduced. The operators chosen-namely the weighted averaging and weighted geometric operators-are fundamental to fuzzy set theory. Their selection is deliberate: as the primary novelty of this study is the introduction of the 2DLPFV itself, utilizing these well-understood operators allows for a clear and unambiguous demonstration of the 2DLPFS’s properties and mechanics. This approach establishes a necessary baseline for the 2DLPFV framework, with the understanding that more advanced operators can be developed in future work.

Averaging aggregation operators

In this section, we will propose some 2-dimension linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy averaging aggregation operators for 2DLPFVs, which include the 2-dimension linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy weighted averaging (2DLPFWA) operator and the 2-dimension linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy ordered weighted averaging (2DLPFOWA) operator.

Definition 4.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_i=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_i}},\dot{s}_{\sigma _{{\tilde{r}}_i}}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_i}},\ddot{h}_{\eta _{\tilde{r}_i}}\right\rangle \right) \ (i=1,2,\dots ,n)$$\end{document} be a collection of 2DLPFVs. Then the 2-dimension linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy weighted averaging (2DLPFWA) operator is a mapping \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2DLPFWA:\Omega ^n\rightarrow \Omega$$\end{document}, such that9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} 2DLPFWA({\tilde{r}}_1,{\tilde{r}}_2,\dots ,{\tilde{r}}_n)=\omega _1\tilde{r}_1\oplus \omega _2{\tilde{r}}_2\oplus \dots \oplus \omega _n{\tilde{r}}_n \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega =(\omega _1,\omega _2,\dots ,\omega _n)^T$$\end{document} is the weight vector of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_i\ (i=1,2,\dots ,n)$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \omega _i\le 1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{i=1}^n{\omega _i}=1$$\end{document}. Especially, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega =(1/n,1/n,\dots ,1/n)^T$$\end{document}, the 2DLPFWA operator would reduce to 2DLPFA operator as:10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} 2DLPFA({\tilde{r}}_1,{\tilde{r}}_2,\dots ,\tilde{r}_n)=\frac{1}{n}\left( \oplus _{i=1}^n{{\tilde{r}}_i}\right) \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Theorem 4.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_i=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_i}},\dot{s}_{\sigma _{{\tilde{r}}_i}}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_i}},\ddot{h}_{\eta _{\tilde{r}_i}}\right\rangle \right) \ (i=1,2,\dots ,n)$$\end{document} be a collection of n 2DLPFVs, then the aggregated value obtained by the 2DLPFWA operator is still a 2DLPFV and is given by11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} 2DLPFWA({\tilde{r}}_1,{\tilde{r}}_2,\dots ,{\tilde{r}}_n)=&\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{t\sqrt{1-\prod _{i=1}^n{\left( 1-\frac{\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_i}^2}{t^2}\right) ^{\omega _i}}}},\dot{s}_{t\prod _{i=1}^n{\left( \frac{\sigma _{{\tilde{r}}_i}}{t}\right) ^{\omega _i}}}\right\rangle ,\right. \\&\left. \left\langle \ddot{h}_{\tau \sqrt{1-\prod _{i=1}^n{\left( 1-\frac{\zeta _{\tilde{r}_i}^2}{\tau ^2}\right) ^{\omega _i}}}},\ddot{h}_{\tau \prod _{i=1}^n{\left( \frac{\eta _{\tilde{r}_i}}{\tau }\right) ^{\omega _i}}}\right\rangle \right) \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega =(\omega _1,\omega _2,\dots ,\omega _n)^T$$\end{document} is the weight vector of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_i\ (i=1,2,\dots ,n)$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \omega _i\le 1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{i=1}^n{\omega _i}=1$$\end{document}.

Proof

Definition 4.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_i=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_i}},\dot{s}_{\sigma _{{\tilde{r}}_i}}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_i}},\ddot{h}_{\eta _{\tilde{r}_i}}\right\rangle \right) \ (i=1,2,\dots ,n)$$\end{document} be a collection of 2DLPFVs. Then the 2-dimension linguistic Pythagorean fuzzy ordered weighted averaging (2DLPFOWA) operator is a mapping \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2DLPFOWA:\Omega ^n\rightarrow \Omega$$\end{document}, such that19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} 2DLPFWA({\tilde{r}}_1,{\tilde{r}}_2,\dots ,{\tilde{r}}_n)=\omega _1\tilde{r}_{(1)}\oplus \omega _2{\tilde{r}}_{(2)}\oplus \dots \oplus \omega _n\tilde{r}_{(n)} \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_{(i)}$$\end{document} is the ith largest value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{r}_1,{\tilde{r}}_2,\dots ,{\tilde{r}}_n$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega =(\omega _1,\omega _2,\dots ,\omega _n)^T$$\end{document} is the associated weight vector of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_{(i)}\ (i=1,2,\dots ,n)$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \omega _i\le 1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{i=1}^n{\omega _i}=1$$\end{document}.

Theorem 4.3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_i=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_i}},\dot{s}_{\sigma _{{\tilde{r}}_i}}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_i}},\ddot{h}_{\eta _{\tilde{r}_i}}\right\rangle \right) \ (i=1,2,\dots ,n)$$\end{document} be a collection of n 2DLPFVs, then the aggregated value obtained by the 2DLPFOWA operator is still a 2DLPFV and is given by20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} 2DLPFOWA({\tilde{r}}_1,{\tilde{r}}_2,\dots ,{\tilde{r}}_n)=&\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{t\sqrt{1-\prod _{i=1}^n{\left( 1-\frac{\theta _{\tilde{r}_{(i)}}^2}{t^2}\right) ^{\omega _i}}}},\dot{s}_{t\prod _{i=1}^n{\left( \frac{\sigma _{\tilde{r}_{(i)}}}{t}\right) ^{\omega _i}}}\right\rangle ,\right. \\&\left. \left\langle \ddot{h}_{\tau \sqrt{1-\prod _{i=1}^n{\left( 1-\frac{\zeta _{\tilde{r}_{(i)}}^2}{\tau ^2}\right) ^{\omega _i}}}},\ddot{h}_{\tau \prod _{i=1}^n{\left( \frac{\eta _{\tilde{r}_{(i)}}}{\tau }\right) ^{\omega _i}}}\right\rangle \right) \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega =(\omega _1,\omega _2,\dots ,\omega _n)^T$$\end{document} is the associated weight vector of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_{(i)}\ (i=1,2,\dots ,n)$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \omega _i\le 1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{i=1}^n{\omega _i}=1$$\end{document}.

Proof

The proof is similar to Theorem 4.1.

Similar to Theorem 4.2, the 2DLPFOWA operator has the following properties: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Theorem 4.4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_i=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_i}},\dot{s}_{\sigma _{{\tilde{r}}_i}}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\zeta _{{\tilde{r}}_i}},\ddot{h}_{\eta _{\tilde{r}_i}}\right\rangle \right) \ (i=1,2,\dots ,n)$$\end{document} be a collection of 2DLPFVs, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega =(\omega _1,\omega _2,\dots ,\omega _n)^T$$\end{document} is the weight vector of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_i\ (i=1,2,\dots ,n)$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \omega _i\le 1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{i=1}^n{\omega _i}=1$$\end{document}, then the 2DLPFOWA operator has the following:

(P1) (Idempotency) If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_i=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{\theta },\dot{s}_{\sigma }\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\zeta },\ddot{h}_{\eta }\right\rangle \right)$$\end{document} for all i, then21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} 2DLPFOWA({\tilde{r}}_1,{\tilde{r}}_2,\dots ,{\tilde{r}}_n)=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{\theta \begin{document}$${\tilde{l}}_i=\left( \left\langle \dot{s}_{\theta _{{\tilde{l}}_i}},\dot{s}_{\sigma _{\tilde{l}_i}}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \ddot{h}_{\zeta _{{\tilde{l}}_i}},\ddot{h}_{\eta _{{\tilde{l}}_i}}\right\rangle \right) \ (i=1,2,\dots ,n)$$\end{document} be another collection of 2DLPFVs such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}_i\le {\tilde{l}}_i$$\end{document} for all i, then22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} 2DLPFOWA({\tilde{r}}_1,{\tilde{r}}_2,\dots ,{\tilde{r}}_n)\le \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{r}}^-=\left( \left\langle \min \{s_{\theta _{{\tilde{r}}_i}}\},\max \{s_{\sigma _{\tilde{r}_i}}\}\right\rangle ,\left\langle \min \{h_{\zeta _{\tilde{r}_i}}\},\max \{h_{\eta _{{\tilde{r}}_i}}\}\right\rangle \right)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek}
